[trialAccount t18501125-145]
145. ELLEN COLLINS and RICHARD COLLINS , unlawfully' uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE HENRY ALLWAY . I am shopman to Messrs. Tomlinson and Budd, pawnbrokers at Barking. Between six and seven o'clock in the evening of 1st Nov., the two prisoners came to the shop; the woman tendered me these two half-crowns in payment for some articles—I told her they were bad, and there was something said about the interest on the articles being 6 d. more—Mr. Budd came into the shop—I saw a bag in the woman's hand, I seized it and turned it out; there were four bad shillings in it, and some good money—Mr. Budd sent for a policeman, and
the prisoners were given in charge—I marked the money in the presence of the policeman, and gave it to him.
WILLIAM BUDD . I was in the shop—I heard a dispute with this woman, and I found two bad half-crowns—I said, "These are bad, where did you get them from?"—she said she got them from some young woman—my young man took the bag and shook out the contents—there were four bad shillings in it, and some good money—the woman said, "If you don't like the two half-crowns, give them me back"—I said I should not; I sent for the officer, and gave them into custody.
HENRY MILSTEAD ( police-sergeant, K 12). I was sent for to Mr. Budd's; and the prisoners were given into my charge—I received these two half-crowns and four shillings—they were marked and given to me—Richard Collins said, "I don't know anything of it; I don't know how she came by them."
WILLIAM WEBSTER . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These coins are all bad, and the shillings are from the same mould.
Ellen Collins's Defence. A young woman asked me to mind this money for her, and said, "If you want to lay out any, you can;" I said I had some things in pawn I wanted to pay for; I took 13 s., and put it in the bag where her money was; I did not know it was bad.
Richard Collins's Defence. The woman came into my house, and gave my wife the money, unknown to me; we do not know where she is.
WILLIAM BUDD re-examined. They mentioned the name of Naney Car-rotty, as the person they received it from—I do not know such a person—I asked where she was, they said they did not know—the prisoners have been customers of mine for three or four years—I never heard anything against them—the man did farming work, and used to buy and sell rabbit skins.
ELLEN COLLINS — GUILTY . Aged 40.
RICHARD COLLINS — GUILTY . Aged 30.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Three Months .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18501125-146]
146. JOHN HOWARD , stealing 1 work-box, and other articles, value 1 l.; the goods of William Anderton.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
MARTHA ANDERTON . I am the wife of William Anderton, he keeps a coffee-shop and eating-house, at Stratford. On 16th Nov. the prisoner came to the shop at twelve o'clock at night, with a young woman—they sat down and had some coffee—in a short time the young woman went out in the back-yard—shortly after she had gone, I missed the prisoner, and I heard some persons outside talking loudly—I sent a woman to tell them to come in, and the young woman came in—she had not a shawl on then, but she had when she first came—she wanted to go out for it, and I said the young man would bring it in with him, and I would not allow her to go out again—I then opened the back-door, and asked him to come in—he was then standing opposite the parlour window—I was then standing with my back to the parlour-door, to prevent the girl from going out, and I heard a noise in the back parlour—I went in with a lighted candle, and found the shutters were open, and the sash lifted up—I saw the prisoner standing outside the room, opposite the window—he had this work-box partly outside the window—I took it from him; it is my husband's property—it contained some thread and thimbles, and other articles—the
shutters of that room had been closed about six o'clock in the evening—I took the box from the prisoner's hand; he escaped—I gave information, and he was taken afterwards—I had seen the box on a small work-table just under the window, perhaps two hours before—a person like the prisoner could have put his hand in that window and reached the box where it was—the prisoner came to my house again in about a quarter of an hour with the girl—I do not think be said anything then—on the Sunday morning he came and said he was very sorry for what he had done; he said I had not lost any thing, and what good would it be to me to imprison him for two or three months—I said, it was no goodness of his that I had lost nothing.
Cross-examined by MR. COCKLE. Q. When you got back the box it was closed? A. Yes; there was a pencil-case in it, and several little things that I use—I know the girl had a shawl taken from her, as she said —I did not know her name—she had a shawl when she went into the yard, and not when she returned—the noise I heard was not of opening the window, it was the things falling out of the work-box—I did not see this man getting out of the window; his arm was in it—he escaped—it was twelve o'clock on Saturday that this occurred.
COURT. Q. Was the prisoner in liquor or not? A. He had just come from a public-house—I think he was a little in liquor—I found some things out of my box—I picked up two silver thimbles, and a penknife.
JAMES RANDALL ( policeman, K. 324) About one o'clock in the morning I was called by Mrs. Anderton who gave him into my custody for stealing the work-box—as I was taking him to the station he made his escape—he was afterwards taken by sergeant Manning, who is not here.
COURT to MARTHA ANDERTON. Q. Who was in your house besides yourself? A. Three or four other men—my husband was not at home—I had a woman assisting me—the prisoner had been at my house several times on a Saturday evening.
( The prisoner received a good character.) GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined One Month
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18501125-147]
147. ROBERT HOLLAND , robbery with violence on Ann Weir, and stealing 1 watch, and other articles, value 12 l.; the goods of Edward Weir.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
ANN WEIR . I am the wife of Edward Weir, who lives in Windmill-lane, Stratford, in the parish of West Ham—I know the prisoner—on Monday evening, 4th Nov., I was in the back kitchen about eight o'clock—there is a door which opens from that to a street by the side of the house—I had a gold chain round my neck—there were two seats and a key, and a ring attached to it, and a silver watch—I had a brooch in my gown—I had the candle in my hand—I walked to the safe to get out the supper—the side door was ajar—I did not see the prisoner—he came in, and came behind me—he said, "You b——, you shall pay for all"—he took hold of me by the throat, took the brooch from my gown, and the watch and seals, and chain from my neck—the chain must have broken—I did not hear anything fall, but they were all gone from me—the prisoner had the watch in his hand, and he was going out—I screamed "Murder!" and my husband stopped him, and took the watch from him—I afterwards
saw the two seals, and key, and ring found against the side door, and the prisoner's cap; it must be his cap, no other person was there—there was no cap lying there before he came—I have seen the watch and chain, and other things since, but I have not seen the brooch—they were worth about 12 l.
Cross-examined by MR. COCKLE. Q. What was the value of the brooch? A. It cost 25 s.; I have had it three years—I was about two yards from the street—the door was ajar, but he must have opened it to come in—he said I should pay for all—I have known him some time—there was a light there—my husband came in as the prisoner was going out—I know the prisoner's mother and family; they have been in the habit of dealing with me, and still owe me money, I have not applied to them for it—I have not had differences with them—I have never had occasion to summon any of his family for an assault—his mother was sent to prison for seven days for an assault on my servant—his sister had fourteen days for striking me on the breast—there was no one in my house that night—my shop was closed—I did not see Mary Collins with the prisoner—the street from the door where he came in, is not a very public street—it is a very low street—I cannot say whether the prisoner was in a very violent state, or whether he was drunk or sober—he was not dragged away by a woman, who said, "Do not make a fool of yourself"—there was no one with him—my servant gave information to the police—I gave information to a man not fit to be in that situation at all—the first policeman I gave information to would not take him; it was William Manning, he would not take him, he was frightened—I told him I was robbed—I did not at first wish to give him in charge for an assault—I did not say so to Manning—he did not say to me, as he had not seen the assault committed he could not take him—he did not say the best plan would be to take out a summons for the assault—I spoke to him about the robbery—I know Mary Donohue—I know her to be a thief—I did not speak to her that night—I never spoke to her till the next morning—I did not say to anybody that it was very fortunate I did not lose my watch and chain, and thank God it was not broken or injured—my husband did not come into the room he took the prisoner in the street, and they both fell down—I was in the back kitchen—I knew where the prisoner lived—when I was first assaulted I sreamed "Murder!" and called for assistance—the scuffle only lasted a few minutes.
EDWARD WEIR . I am the husband of the last witness. On Monday evening, 4th Nov., about eight o'clock, I was standing at my front door—there is a door from my back kitchen which leads to a bye street—I heard my wife scream, and I ran to the side-door—I saw the prisoner coming out, I laid hold of him, and took from him my wife's gold guard-chain and silver watch—he had them in his right hand—I called, "Police!" and endeavoured to detain him—he resisted, and tried to get away—I laid hold of him, and struggled with him—we both tumbled down together—he at last got away, before the police were there—I examined the back kitchen, and found near the door the two seals, and key, and ring, which my wife was in the habit of wearing—my wife had a brooch that night, which has never been found since—I saw a cap near the door—I do not know whose it was—I know the prisoner—this is the watch, and guard, and seals, and key, and ring—they are all my property.
Cross-examined. Q. Who came up besides you? A. My servant-maid; she is here—no one else came up—I went into the room after this —there was no light there—there was a lamp outside, so that I could see distinctly—I knew the prisoner well, and knew where he lived—the charge was first made against him to Manning; I told him to take him, for I had been robbed—he refused to take him—he did not say I had better take out a summons—he was on duty—the prisoner was taken on the Tuesday evening, by the officer who is here—the prisoner had to pass my shop on his way home from work.
MR. RYLAND. Q. When you had hold of the prisoner, you called, "Police?" A. Yes; no policeman came—Manning was standing about thirty yards off; he did not come to the spot, I went to him, and he said he would not go down that street for any one—Benton then came up and he took the charge.
JOSEPH BENTON ( policeman, K 381). I was about forty yards from the house—the prosecutor came running to me, and told me what had happened—he had this watch and chain with him—he said they had been taken from his wife's neck—I went back with him to the house—I searched the kitchen, and saw the seal, key, and ring found about half a yard from the door—it is but a narrow kitchen—the servant picked up this cap, in my presence, just inside the door; I know it is the prisoner's—I stopped in the house about five minutes—I then went away to look for the prisoner—I saw him, and he ran into a house, and ran into three or four houses, which are like a rabbit-warren—I am certain he saw me—I did not call to him; but some persons called out that I was coming, and he ran away—he had no cap on—I took him the next day, by Stratford-gate, about three-quarters of a mile from the prosecutor's, about a quarter before six o'clock in the evening—I told him what I took him for—he said he knew nothing about it; he had not been there—he was about four or five houses from the prosecutor's when I saw him after this matter happened—he was standing about the middle of the street—I had known him before.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was it you found out this? A. About eight o'clock; I saw the prisoner in the street about five or ten minutes afterwards—I did not know which house he lived in—I have seen him wear a cap, and when I apprehended him he said, "You have got my cap, I hear"—I said, "You have got a cap on now; what do you want with another?"—he said that was only a borrowed one.
MARY RYAN . I am servant to Mr. and Mrs. Weir. I remember hearing Mrs. Weir had been robbed—I went for the supper beer, and came back just after this happened—I saw the things found by the door—I picked up the cap, and said, " Here is the prisoner's cap."
GUILTY of an Assault. †Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.

[trialAccount t18501125-149]
149. JAMES MANBY and MARY MANBY , stealing 2 spoons, value 10 s.; the goods of the President, Vice-Presidents, Treasurer, and Governors of the Infant Orphan Asylum.
MARY SHIPP . I am matron of the Infant Orphan Asylum. On 16th Oct. I returned to the Asylum, having been absent, and in consequence of what I was told, I missed a silver teaspoon with "I. O. A." on the front and "8" on the back of it—I believe these pieces ( produced) to be part of that spoon—there are the letters "I. O. A." on one piece and "8" on another—I only missed one spoon then, I have missed one since—by the counting of the spoons there are two missing—they are the property of the Trustees of the Infant Orphan Asylum.
Cross-examined by MR. M. PRENDERGAST. Q. When did you leave the Asylum? A. On 5th Aug.—I cannot say I counted the spoons when I left, but I believe they were right, they were reported to me as being right—I did not miss any till the policeman produced the pieces—the prisoner James has been in the habit of coming daily to the Asylum for the last two years, to take away pigs' wash.
COURT. Q. Had you seen him on the premises before you went away? A. Yes; and after I came back—he was not employed by the Asylum.
HENRY JOHN WAYLAND . I am a silversmith at Stratford. On 5th Nov. the female prisoner and another woman came to my shop and asked if I purchased old silver—I said, "Yes;" and the prisoner produced these pieces of silver spoons—I examined them, and told her I had no doubt they had been stolen—she said her husband had found them in some wash—I took the pieces and told them I must inquire further about them, and they must call the next day—the part with the initials was so bent that I could not see them—I bent it right, went after them, and gave them into custody, and gave the pieces to Benton, who I had sent for—the female prisoner gave the name of "Jury, of Chingford."
JOSEPH BENTON ( policeman K, 381). I was sent for to Mr. Wayland's and found the female prisoner and another female there—they were given into my custody, and I received the pieces of silver from Mr. Wayland—the female prisoner said her husband had found them in some dung wash—they were taken to the station and searched, and the female searcher gave me a silver knife found on the other woman—I afterwards went to the Infant Orphan Asylum, saw the matron, and produced the pieces to her—I afterwards apprehended James Manby, and told him it was on suspicion of stealing two silver spoons—he said he found them in turning some dung over in the Forest—I asked him whether they were whole or broken up—he said, "Whole"—I produce the Act of Parliament, which I got from the secretary, Mr. Buckler.
Cross-examined. Q. Was any one present when he said they were whole? A. No—he did not say they were in pieces—the prisoners have been out on bail in consequence of the female having a child.
( The prisoners received good characters.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Russell Gurney. Esq.
[trialAccount t18501125-150]
150. CATHERINE FITZGERALD and BRIDGET DONOVAN , stealing 1 50 l. bank-note; the property of Samuel Vain, her master, in his dwelling-house. 2nd COUNT charging DONOVAN with receiving.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL VAIN . I am a butcher, of Barking. Fitzgerald was in my service above five weeks—I tock her on an emergency, without inquiring about her character—she was the only servant, except a man who slept in
the house—on 27th Oct. I missed a 50 l. note from a pocket-book in a drawer in my bedroom—I had received it in Feb. from Mr. Spaskett—I saw it there in the early part of Oct.—the key was kept in a closet in the room we live in down-stairs—the man-servant does not go into that room—I sent for a policeman and gave Fitzgerald in charge—she said she knew nothing about it—on Sunday, 13th Oct., she went out to go to church—she came back at the usual hour—on 14th she asked leave to go out—she went, and returned in an hour—on 15th, Hawkins, her sister, came for her to go out—her mistress let her go—she came home about half-past eight—her mistress told her she had been drinking—she said she had only had a glass of porter—she looked red in the face.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did not the policeman tell you she had said that she had been out on Sunday? A. No; I knew it—he did not tell me he had spoken to her before—I had four 10 l. notes in that drawer as well with the 50 l.—they were left.
HENRY MILSTEAD ( police-sergeant, K 12). On 29th Oct. I was called to Mr. Vain—he gave Fitzgerald in charge for stealing a 50 l.-note—she said she knew nothing of it—at the station the female searcher brought her some tea and said, "What a bad woman you must be to rob your master in that way"—she said, "I am sure I did not take it, the woman who assists me in charring has got the run of the house, she must have taken it and dropped it in the street, and my brother must have picked it up"—I had cautioned her—next morning going to the office she said her brother, Fitzgerald, her sister, Donovan, and herself, had been to her sister's at Barking, and when she returned her mistress accused her of drinking.
WILLIAM FRENCH . I am a pawnbroker, of Poplar, which is four miles from Barking. On 14th Oct. Donovan came, and took two articles out of pledge, amounting to 17 s. 4 d.—I believe they had been pledged by her sister Hawkins—she tendered me a new 50 l. -note—I asked, how she got it—she said some person from Barking had sent her with it to take the articles out—I told her to take it back, and tell the parties to come themselves—next day Fitzgerald's sister-in-law came.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw nothing of Fitzgerald? A. No.
WILLIAM SKINNER . I keep the Ship, Poplar. On 14th Oct., about six o'clock in the evening, Donovan came, and called for three gallons of beer and half a gallon of rum—she presented this 50 l.-note ( produced), and said she had it from her cousin, who had just come from sea—I said I had got no change—I let her have 10 l.—she left the note with me, and was to come next day for the balance—she came and had it, leaving 5 l. in my hands, which I have now—I wrote my name and address on it.
JOSEPH PUDDIFORD ( policeman, K 276). On Sunday, 27th Oct., I took Donovan to Mr. Skinner's—she was not exactly in custody—he said she was the party who brought him the note—she said if I would go with her to her brother Mike Sullivan, at West Ham, he would tell me where he got it—we went, and she picked out a house as her brother's—I knocked; she said, "Don't knock any more; I have made a mistake, it is further along"—she pointed out another house; I knocked, and a man and woman came—I asked if a sailor lived there; they said no, and he never had—I asked if they knew the prisoner; they said only by seeing her in the market-place, selling apples—she said, "The young man
promised to meet me here to-night"—I said, "Now where shall we go?" she said, "Back to Poplar"—we went, and she said, "Give me the law for it, that is all you can do now."
CATHERINE FIELDER . I search females at the station. I searched Donovan, she took these eighteen sovereigns from her bosom in a bag, put them on a seat, and put her clothes on them—I found it, and she said, "Do not tell the policeman I had any money about me"—I opened the bag, and said, "They are sovereigns, where did you get it?"—she said, "My sister's son has come home from sea, and has given me a 50 l.-note."
RICHARD ADEY BAILEY . I am a clerk in the Bank of England. I produce this note—it was brought in on 15th Oct., in exchange for gold—it has on it "W. Skinner, 103, High-street, Poplar."
WILLIAM TAYLOR . I am a clerk in the Bank of England. This 50.-note, 17137, dated 6th Dec, 1849, was issued, with five others, consecutive numbers, on 18th Jan., 1850, to a person named Spaskett.
RICHARD ADEY BAILEY re-examined. I have ascertained that the note traced to the prisoner is the only one left of the six—all the others have been paid in three months ago.
CHRISTOPHER SPASKETT . In Jan. last I received six 50 l. -notes at the Bank of England—I paid one of them to Mr. Vain.
Cross-examined. Q. You took no note of the persons to whom you paid the others? A. No.
SAMUEL VAIN re-examined. A woman used to come to assist Fitzgerald in washing, in the kitchen, quite apart from the room where the key or the money was—she had not the run of the house—my wife was always about.
Donovan's Defence. All the money I had I got from the sailor, my sister's son.
FITZGERALD — GUILTY of Stealing. Aged 30.— Confined Twelve
Months.
DONOVAN — GUILTY of Receiving. Aged 27.— Confined Nine Months.

152. CATHERINE GARSON , stealing 71bs. weight of tobacco, value 1 l. 4 s.; the goods of William Phillips, her master: to which she pleaded

[trialAccount t18501125-153]
153. JANE MOULDS , stealing 1 petticoat, and other articles, value 2 l., and 1 sovereign, and 1 half-sovereign; the property of John Bull, in his dwelling-house.
ANN BULL . I am the wife of John Bull; I live at Woolwich, and occasionally let lodgings. On Tuesday night, 22nd Oct., the prisoner was brought to my house about half-past nine o'clock, by a person who lived next door, where she had taken supper—she asked for a lodging—I asked her if she was a stranger in Woolwich—she said, "Yes," that she had come from London with her husband, who was gone to Burrage-grove to sit up with a brother who had died—I showed her up to a room—she had the room to herself—there was a chest of drawers there containing wearing-apparel, a purse containing a sovereign, and a half-sovereign, and another purse containing my certificate, and the receipt for my rent—I had put those things in the drawers the day before, and locked them up—the prisoner did not come down in the morning, and I went up about eleven, and asked if anything was the matter—she answered that she was getting up—I went down-stairs for I dare say two hours; she did not come down—I went up again at half-past two—I knocked, and told her she must open the door, which she unlocked, and let me in—the stairs do not open to the street, but inside the shop—no one could have gone into that room that night or day without my knowing it—there are three or four locks before you get in that room—a gentleman named Howe lodged in my house, and William Lawrence, a carpenter—my husband is at sea—when the prisoner let me into the room, I asked her what was the matter that she had been there all day—she said she had been waiting for her husband to come from the Burrage-grove, and he had not come, and she had not any money to pay for the bed—there was a bundle on the table; I asked her whose it was—she said it was hers—it was wrapped up in a handkerchief of hers—I told her she must leave the bundle till she paid for her lodging—she hesitated a good bit, and then asked if it would be safe—I told her it would be quite safe for her to leave it with me—she said she would go to the Burrage-grove to her husband to get money to pay for her bed, and come back for her bundle—when she was gone, I examined the bundle; I found it contained a number of my things, which had been in the drawers, but not the purses—the locks were all broken—I missed the two purses, and the sovereign and a half, two pairs of trowsers, a waistcoat-piece not made up, a petticoat, and two towels—they were not in the bundle—I have never seen them since—the window of the room the prisoner was in, opens to the street; it is on the first-floor—no one had occupied that room after I had seen the things safe the day before—I told my uncle—he gave information—he had been stopping in my house for three weeks—I saw the prisoner again in about half an hour after she went away, in the street, coming back to my house—my uncle brought her back—a policeman came in, directly after her; I do not think she saw him—I was present when she was searched at the station—she was wearing a petticoat of mine, and the shift was rolled up like a bussel behind her—she might have had the trowsers on when she left—I neither saw nor heard any other person in my house—I slept in the back parlour—the lodgers are there still—these things ( produced) are all mine.
REBECCA JACKSON . I am the wife of a policeman. I searched the prisoner at the Woolwich station—I found this petticoat on her, and this shift, which was tied on her as a bussel—Mrs. Bull claimed, them—in other respects the prisoner was poorly dressed.
ROBERT HILL ( police-sergeant, R 42). I received information, went to the house, and found these things tied up in a bundle—Mrs. Bull gave
charge of the prisoner for stealing them, also two pairs of trowsers, and some other things, and a sovereign and a half—the drawers all seemed to have been forced by something like a chisel.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-154]
154. ELLEN BROADBENT and MARIA MURRAY , stealing 16 yards of silk and other articles, value 8 l. 9 s.; the goods of Mary Ann McArthur, the mistress of Broadbent: to which
BROADBENT pleaded GUILTY . Aged 26.— She received a good
character.— Judgment Respited.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman, R 122). I went to Murray's house—Broadbent was lodging with her—I searched the house, and found all these articles in Murray's room ( produced)—Murray told me it was her room, and she was present when I searched it—I found part of this property in the drawers, the hearth-rug on the floor, and part of the property in a band-box, which Murray said was hers—another bandbox was produced from Mrs. Brown's—she came in and said, "I want one of you"—at the time I found the goods I saw no other band-box there—the officer Newell brought in a bandbox, and Murray said, "It is that bandbox that I took into the next house; I found it under the bed; I did not think there was any harm in taking it there, as the constable did not search it"—another constable had searched the premises before me—directly Newell came in with the box, Murray said, "That is the box; I did not know what was in it"—I said to Murray, "Have you got any property in your house that does not belong to you?"—she said, "No, I have not," and then I said, "I must search your house"—I showed her the things I found, and asked her if they were her things, and she said they were—she pulled them out of the drawers herself, and told me they were hers—I found this spoon in a cupboard in Murray's room—I asked her, and she said it was hers—that was after the corded box was brought in.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Broadbent had been taken into custody before this? A. Yes; there were other articles in the drawers—I believe Murray only occupies one room; her husband lives with her there—I heard that Broadbent had gone from her mistress' there to be confined—there were two bandboxes and things in drawers
MARY ANN MCARTHUR . I am the wife of John McArthur. Broadbent was in my service—I missed a number of articles while she was with me—these things found in the box that Murray said was hers, are mine—this spoon is mine, it has the initials of my maiden name on it, "Mary Ann Edwards"—it was in this state when I lost it—the letters are nearly rubbed out.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had Broadbent been in your service?
A. Rather better than six months—here is no mark on this habit-shirt, or on this apron found in Murray's box—I know they are mine, and this piece of print—these other articles found in the box that was corded I have no doubt about—these tablecloths, and other things, are mine, they have my initials.
JOHN NEWELL ( policeman, R 340). I went to Murray's-house and apprehended Broadbent—I searched a box she pointed out to me, and found in it the property that Mrs. M'Arthur has identified—that was about an hour before Gladwin searehed the box was in Murray's room, and was pointed out by Broadbent.
COURT. Q. Was Murray there? A. Yes; she knew that Broad-bent had been taken—I did not see any bandbox—I went there when Gladwin searched—I then saw a bandbox on the drawers, I heard Gladwin ask Murray if it was her's; she said, "Yes," it was all her property that she had in the room—Mrs. Brown came in, and said she wanted one of us, there was something taken into her washhouse which she feared would get her into trouble—I went and fetched a bandbox—Murray said that was the bandbox she had taken in, and she did not think any harm of doing so—I saw Gladwin find the spoon in the cupboard, which was open—he showed it to Murray, and said, "Is this yours?"—she said, "Yes; I wonder you should look about the place like that"—there were other spoons there—there was a pencil-case, and these other pieces of old silver; Gladwin showed them to Murray in his hand, and she said they were all her own.
MRS. M'ARTHUR re-examined. These are all mine—I know them by the mark.
Cross-examined. Q. Here is a thimble? A. Yes; there is part of a silver spoon, I am sure it is mine—this part of a spoon has "M. R" on it; they are not my initials—I lost a piece of silver of this shape—this part of a spoon is mine; I lost the other piece—I lost at the same time this pencil-case and thimble—they were kept in a teapot with the spoon—Broadbent had access to every place—the value of all I lost was about 8 l. MURRAY received a good character. NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18501125-155]
155. JOHN PINGRUM and JOHN FIELDER , stealing 60 pairs of clogs, value 2 l.; the goods of Henry Deadman.
ELIZABETH GARRATT . I am single, and know Mr. Deadman, a clog-maker, of Tanner's-hill, Deptford. I attended on his mother—I have occasionally taken orders from him—on 24th Oct., about ten o'clock, I saw the prisoners in the shop, Pingrum was behind the counter, and Fielder on the customer's side; there was a female in the shop, and an old lady who had sent for me—Fielder had lived with Mr. Deadman, and I knew Pingrum—I saw him take a basket of clogs from behind the counter—they each took a basket—I asked what they were going to do; they said Mr. Deadman had sent them, and they were going to take them away—they each went out with a basket of clogs; Mr. Deadman came home, and I told him.
Pingrum. Q. It is not the first time you have given me clogs. A. I have given them to you both when Mr. Deadman ordered me; and I have received the money from Fielder.
HENRY DEADMAN . I am a clog-maker, of Deptford. Fielder was in
my service up to 19th Oct.—he used to hawk clogs about the country for me—I had discharged him on 19th Oct., because I found him dishonest—I have employed Pingrum in the same way at different times—I did not employ either of them on 24th Oct.—I came home that evening, and missed five dozen clogs, worth two guineas—I saw them safe at nine o'clock that morning—the prisoners had applied to me on the Wednesday before for some more clogs, and I told them I would have nothing more to do with them—I gave them no order to receive these clogs—I have not seen them since—when I employed them they used to bring me the money every evening.
Fielder. Q. Did I not meet you in Church-street on 24th Oct., and ask you to let me have some clogs? A. No, you had not been out with clogs for me that day.
Pingrum. We met you on 24th Oct. in Church-street, and you asked us if our baskets were ready as usual. Witness. I did not.
ISAAC GARRATT ( policeman, N 149). I took the prisoners together at Deptford on 29th Oct., and told them the charge—Pingrum said he was hawking a basket of clogs, and going up Goodham-lane, in Kent, a man asked him where his license was, and said some hawkers were coming, and as he was hawking without a license he threw the clogs in the ditch and ran away—Fielder said the same.
HENRY DEADMAN re-examined. I did not supply them with any license—after I had been to the station on the Saturday night they sent my baskets home—I saw them on Sunday; they did not tell me a word about throwing the clogs away—they wanted to say something, but I said I had put it out of my power, and refused to listen—it is not true that I met them in Church-street.
Pingrum. Q. Did not I give you 6 s. 1 d. on Wednesday evening? A. No; I did not tell you if you did not get the money in two or three days you would have to run again; these clogs were packed to go to Bromley.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18501125-156]
156. WILLIAM EVERETT and THOMAS MARLOW were indicted (with BENJAMIN MARLOW , not in custody), for feloniously cutting and wounding George Frampton, with intent to resist the lawful apprehension of the said Thomas Marlow.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE FRAMPTON ( policeman, R 253). On Sunday morning, 6th Oct., about ten minutes past twelve o'clock at night, I was on duty with Saunders in the Lewisham-road, and saw the prisoners making a noise—Marlow came up to me, looked me hard in the face, and said, "It is you, you b——r, is it?"—I said, "Yes, it is me"—I told him he had better go home, for I did not want any disturbance there—he said he would not go home, he had served one month at Maidstone, and the Magistrate told him we had no business to interfere with him—I told him to go home several times—they kept swearing and abusing me—I took out my watch, and said, "Now, if you will go home, I don't want to interfere with you; I will give you five minutes to disperse"—I waited five minutes—three of them went away—there were six—the prisoners and Benjamin Marlow stopped—I took hold of Everett gently by the shoulder,
and put him round the corner of Silver-street to go home—he said, "Come on, Tom, we will give it to the b——s to night"—he said they would clear the road of us—they went straight to a faggot-stack up Silver-street, and tried to pull some out, but could not—the prisoners came back—Everett said, "Go it, Tom, I am ready"—they had nothing with them then—Marlow struck me twice in the breast with his fist; Everett and the other man were close by—I took Marlow into custody with my left hand—he dropped down and caught hold of my legs—I took out my staff, and hit him twice on the arm to make him let go—I then received a heavy blow between the shoulders which knocked me down—I tried to handcuff him, but received a blow across my nose and face, which knocked me down senseless—it could not have been given by Thomas Marlow—my face was cut right open—I am in pain now—I have not been able to be on duty since.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. At the time you received the blow, had Marlow got away?" A. No, I was handcuffing him at the time—Everett's brother was there—I believe I gave them the full five minutes—I did not take out my watch again—I had not collared Marlow before he struck me—there were some palings between them and the stack.
BENJAMIN SAUNDERS ( policeman, R 220). On Sunday morning, 6th Oct., a little after twelve o'clock, I was on duty with Frampton, in the Lewisham-road—the prisoners were making a disturbance, and Frampton told them to leave—he took out his watch, and gave them five minutes, at the end of which they ran up to a wood-stack, to draw some sticks, but they did not—Marlow said, "We mean to give it you to-night, you b—r"—we followed them to see where they were gone, and they met Frampton, and Marlow struck him in the breast twice, with his double fist—Frampton then took Marlow by the arm, and Marlow ran his head between Frampton's legs, and tried to throw him down—Everett and Benjamin Marlow then went to a wooden fence, and drew a paling each; Everett threw one at Frampton, which took him athwart the nose and right cheek-bone, and he fell down senseless—they immediately ran away —Frampton bled very much indeed—Marlow was taken a few days afterwards—when Everett threw the paling, Marlow was about three feet from him—he had just got away from Frampton—it was a wooden paling, with square edges, about three feet and a half long, and about an inch and a half thick—he stood about six yards from Frampton at the time he threw it—the middle part of it struck him.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was Benjamin Marlow at the time? A. He stood close by Everett—I was about nine feet from Frampton—the paling went past me—I was between them, only a little on one side—the prisoner Marlow was then at the side of the road, between Frampton and me—Frampton struck Marlow twice across the arm with his staff—I did not hear him cry out—it lasted altogether about five minutes, it might be less.
COURT. Q. Were these men very drunk at the time? A. No.
EDWARD COUCHER . I am a surgeon, at Deptford-bridge. On Sunday morning, 6th Oct., about one o'clock, Frampton was brought to me—he was bleeding considerably from the nose and cheek—the wound on the nose was an irregular-shaped cut; that on the cheek was a sharp, clean cut—a sharp-edged paling would be very likely to cause it—it
must have been a very severe blow—the nasal-bones were broken—he is scarcely out of my hands now—he is about well enough to return to duty.
( The prisoners received good characters.)
EVERETT — GUILTY . Aged 19.
MARLOW — GUILTY . Aged 19.
Confined Two Years.
Before Russell Gurney, Esq.
[trialAccount t18501125-157]
157. CHARLES HARRISON , stealing 3 handkerchiefs, 1 sovereign, 1 half-sovereign, and 10 shillings; the property of Thomas Gibson.
THOMAS GIBSON . I am in the 5th batallion of Royal Artillery, Woolwich. The prisoner was in my department. On 27th Oct., at a quarter to six o'clock, I left the barrack-room, leaving my clothes and money safe in my box—I returned at half-past eight, and found my box broken open, and three handkerchiefs, a sovereign, half-sovereign, half-crowns, shillings, sixpences, and four penny pieces gone—the prisoner slept in that room—he was absent when the roll was called, at half-past eight—next morning I went with Walker to a brothel in Woolwich, and found the prisoner there, in bed, and my three handkerchiefs in his coat-pocket.
Prisoner. Q. Was the door locked when you went out? A. Yes.
JOHN WALKER ( policeman, K 298). I went with Gibson, and found the prisoner in bed with a prostitute—these three handkerchiefs were in his jacket-pocket, and a half-sovereign, two half-crowns, two shillings, and a sixpence between the bed and the mattress.
Prisoner's Defence. I found the handkerchiefs at the door, and put them into my pocket; the money I had I won at cards.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-158]
158. WILLIAM SMITH , feloniously cutting and wounding John Ashton; with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
JOHN ASHTON . I keep the Prince of Wales, Woolwich. On 9th Nov., about half-past eight o'clock, there was some sappers fighting in my room—I went and separated them—the prisoner and three of his comrades were there, but they had nothing to do with the disturbance—they were in the passage—one of them was going into the tap-room as I came out—he swore he would be revenged on some one—I said, "There has been quite disturbance enough, my man; be quiet"—he struck me, and we both went down in the passage—the prisoner and his comrades were outside, striking with sticks—Smith tore down the top half of the door, and entered the bar, and struck my wife with a stick, on the head—his comrades said, "Break the door down, and fetch the b——out; if we get him outside, we will kill him"—I was struck by some one—my wife struck the prisoner with a stick—he took the stick from her, and struck me on the head—my head was cut open—I cannot say whether the prisoner did that—he had been drinking, but was not drunk.
RHODA ASHTON . I am the wife of the last witness. I was in the bar, and heard a noise—three soldiers attacked my husband—one knocked him down—I pulled him into the bar, bolted the door, took the staff down, and told the prisoner if he entered the door, I would knock him down—he dragged the door till he got it open—he pulled the bottom of it to pieces—he entered, and I made a blow at him—he took the staff from
me, and struck my husband—the others rushed in with sticks to drag my husband out—I was hit both by a stick and a staff—I saw my husband's head bleeding profusely—I cannot say which blow caused it.
JOHN WALKER ( policeman, R 298). I took the prisoner—he made his escape for about fifty yards; he fell; I retook him—he had been drinking, but knew what he was about—Ashton was in a dreadful state.
Prisoner's Defence. There were fifty or sixty men fighting; they were pretty nearly all intoxicated.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 24.— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

159. ALFRED FOYLE , stealing 1 watch and guard, take 3 s. 16 s.; the goods of George Harvey: to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-160]
160. JOHN HART and THOMAS HARDY , stealing 1 hat-case, 1 gun, 1 chest of drawers, 3 boxes, 1 pistol, and other articles, value 65 l. 5 s.6 d.; the goods of Matthew Smith Dodsworth.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
CAPTAIN MATTHEW SMITH DODSWORTH . I reside at Thornton-hall, near Bedale, in Yorkshire; I was in the eleventh batallion of Artillery. Hart was my servant six weeks—I left the regiment, I left Woolwich, at the end of Aug.—I remained a short time in London, and then went to Thornton-hall—before leaving, I told Hart to look after my baggage—I afterwards wrote this letter to him, and I received this reply ( produced)—to the best of my belief, this ( produced)letter is Hart's writing; I never saw him write—I never received the property I had left behind me—it was never sent to me in Yorkshire, and, in consequence, I wrote to Captain Cannon—my property consisted of a chest of drawers, containing clothes, a gun and case, a pistol, saddle, a portmanteau and trunk, a carpet bag, some arm-chairs, and furniture of all descriptions, worth about 80 l. or 100 l.—I believe this letter to be Hart's writing, in consequence of the answers it gives, I cannot conceive how anybody else could have written it—I never sent any servant from the Euston Hotel, to Hart for the things, and I never gave any servant of mine an order to Hart to deliver the things—all the orders I gave are contained in that letter of 21st.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. When did you go to Yorkshire? A. At the end of Aug., and returned last Tuesday morning—I was not at the Victoria Hotel, Euston-square, in Oct.—I do not think I had before been absent from the regiment while Hart was my servant—I did not take any servant with me when I left—I have had no servant travelling with me—up to this time I had a high opinion of Hart.
JOHN M'CALLUM . I am quarter-master-sergeant of the 11th battalion of Artillery, at Woolwich. Hart was a gunner and driver in that battalion—I remember Captain Dodsworth leaving about 30th Aug., and I received directions to place his baggage in the store, of which I have the control—I received the packages, consisting of three large boxes, a plain deal case, a gun-case, a bag, and other packages—they remained in my possession till 2nd Oct., when I delivered them to Hart, who said he was going to send them to Captain Dodsworth, in the country—he said he was going to take them to his own house, and I lent him a hand-cart for that purpose
—on the 12th I heard that Captain Dodsworth had not received the baggage—I went and found Hart in the barracks, and told him Captain Cannon wanted to see him—he went with me to the captain, who asked him what he had done with the baggage—he said Captain Dodsworth's servant had called for it on the previous Tuesday; that was 8th Oct., with a light van, and taken it away with him to London—he was asked if he knew him to be Captain Dodsworth's servant—he said he did not know him; that he had brought a note from Captain Dodsworth, desiring the baggage might be given up to him; that Captain Dodsworth was residing at the time at an hotel in Euston-square—Captain Cannon asked him whether he knew the man—he said he thought he should know him—Captain Cannon said to me, in Hart's hearing, perhaps the baggage might be deposited at the hotel in Euston-square at that time, as it was as short a time, and directed me to write to the landlord of the Victoria Tavern—I did so, and received an answer—Captain Cannon asked Hart if he knew Captain Dodsworth's writing, and if he believed the note to be in his writing—he said he believed it was his writing; and the man gave such a description of the difficulties under which Mr. Dodsworth was labouring, that he had no doubt it was right—Captain Cannon asked him what had become of the letter—he said he could not tell; it must have been torn up by the children, or the man must have taken it away that took the baggage away—he said he followed the man with the things some distance, to see whether he would take them by railway or water to London; but it being near his stable-hour, he was obliged to leave, and go to duty.
Cross-examined. Q. Had he not been to you between 21st Sept. and 2nd Oct., and asked you for the luggage to send to Captain Dodsworth? A. He had been previous to the time he got it, but I do not know the date—I refused to give it, unless he could produce satisfactory authority— he said he had received a letter from Captain Dodsworth, but he did not show it to me—I was afterwards ordered to give it up to him, by my commanding-officer; not by Captain Cannon—I do not recollect whether he told Captain Cannon that the light van was procured in Woolwich to take the baggage to the water.
EDWARD JAMES LEVI ( police-inspector, R). On 23rd Oct., in consequence of information from Captain Dodsworth I caused Hart to be apprehended—I saw him when he was brought to the station, and the constable who brought him said, "I bring this man here, on suspicion of stealing a quantity of luggage belonging to Captain Dodsworth"—I asked what he had to say respecting the charge—he told me he had given Captain Dodsworth's luggage to a man who came with a horse and cart with a note purporting to come from Captain Dodsworth—I asked him the description of the man—he said he was between thirty or forty years of age—he said the note was lost or mislaid at the time of the removal of the luggage—I detained him—I have made inquiry at the railway, and endeavoured to trace the luggage but have not been able.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been an inspector? A. Five years—I do not consider that I cross-examined Hart—I considered I was doing my duty in putting the questions.
LEWIS ROWE . I am employed at May's livery stables, Woolwich. On 8th Oct., in the evening, about a quarter to five o'clock, Hart came, and
said he wanted a horse and cart to take some luggage down to the pier—I asked where it was to take up—he said opposite the dead-house—he waited while I put the horse in, and told roe to bring the cart up to the dead-house, and he would look out for me—there was no one with him—I went there, and he came in a little time, and ordered me round to Sandy-hill, with the cart to the back of the house where he lived—it was then close upon five o'clock—he then said, "You and me must get the luggage down-stairs"—we went up and got it down—there might be eight or nine packages, I did not take particular notice—I recollect a gun-case, a carpet-bag, and a bed and mattress, and some very large cases that had furniture in them—we loaded them into the cart, except a hat-box which he carried—when we went to the back door, I said, "Would it not be easier to get them out at the front door"—he said, "No; it was not so handy"—I do not know that the back is more private—I then proceeded down to the pier, and got him to carry the hat-box behind the cart, and see that nothing fell off, and when I got opposite the Sappers and Miners barracks, I saw him in conversation with a man, who the prisoner Hardy resembles very much in height and stoutness, but it was between lights, and I will not undertake to swear to him—I had tied the things very lightly, and when we had got to a certain part, I said I must stop and fasten them up, and Hart said, "No; go on, we want to catch the last boat"—I said, "I must do it"—I undid the horse, and fastened them up as well as I could—after that, the other man asked me to have a drop of beer; I had some with him and Hart, and after that Hart turned the hat-box over to the other man, and said to me, "This man will go with you to the pier," and I got him to look behind, and see that nothing fell out—Hart said he must go to his stable—when we got to the pier the boat was gone, and I put the things down on the wharf—the porter took them of me, and I did not wait—it was then half-past six, or twenty minutes to seven—the man paid me 3 s.
STEPHEN HARWOOD . I am a porter, and lire in Union-street, Woolwich. I remember on the evening of 8th Oct., the last witness bringing some luggage to the pier—Hardy was with him, and said he wanted the things taken down to the boat—I helped to take them on board, and he paid me 1 s. 6 d., and went on board with the luggage—it consisted of a large box, a gun-case, a carpet-bag, and some officers chests—I saw no direction on any of the packages—I have no doubt about its being the luggage Rowe brought—it is not usual for these small steamers to have a heavy lot like that, and 1 s. 6 d. is a very fair job——Hardy went off with the boat to the North Woolwich terminus.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you first see Hardy again? A. About three weeks after, when he came with Gladwin—I did not tell Gladwin he was not the man, or that I believed he was not the man—I did not tell him he was the man—Gladwin did not bring me to him to see him—I saw him as he walked out of the boat—I did not see him again till he was at the police-court—Gladwin told me to go, because I told him I thought he was the man—I do not know whether he had been examined before that—I was there twice.
GEORGE RING . I live on the Essex steam-boat, and belong to the pier. On 8th Oct. I recollect some luggage coming from on board a steamboat that had brought it from Woolwich pier, and was to go to London by train—it consisted of nine or ten packages, officers' boxes, gun-cases,
and so on—Hardy was with it—I have no doubt about him—there was not time to get it on shore for the seven o'clock train, and Hardy waited on the platform till the quarter-past eight train—I walked and talked with him during that time, and drank part of a pint of porter—he said he was short of money and did not know whether he should have enough—he did not say who the luggage belonged to—when he came off the steam-boat he had no ticket—it is part of my duty to look after the tickets, and that called my attention to him—I saw him pay the station-master.
Cross-examined. Q. That is very common, is it not? A. They are supposed to pay before they get on board—I never saw him before—he was dressed as now, and had a cap.
JAMES GIBBONS . I am the railway station-master at North Woolwich—I remember the arrival of twelve or thirteen packages of luggage on 8th Oct.—there was a gun-case and other things—Hardy came with them, and he had to stay till the quarter-past eight train—I saw him and talked to him before the train came—he said he was going to the West-end with the luggage—it weighed 9 cwt. 3qrs.—he paid nine shillings for the 9 cwt: and the 3qrs. he was allowed to take as a passenger—he gave his name Dobson—I have no doubt Hardy is the man—he was there half an hour, but was ten minutes in my office, and his face within a foot of mine.
Cross-examined. Q. You had not seen him before? A. No; some hundreds of people went by the trains in the course of the day, but no one but the prisoner paid me for any luggage—I think it was about ten or twelve days after, that my attention was called to this.
JOHN WALLER . I am a porter, at the North Woolwich station—I remember Hardy coming on 8th Oct. with a quantity of packages by the steamboat from Woolwich—I helped to get them from the boat to the train—Hardy stayed there an hour, during which time we had a pint of porter and talked together—there were ten or twelve packages, officers' chests and gun-cases—Hardy went off with them in the train.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he dressed as now? A. No, he had a cap and jacket on—I had not seen him before to my knowledge—I next saw him when he was brought by Gladwin in the train—Gladwin did not show him to me, but I went to the court—I saw him in the crowd, and recognized him directly—I was not asked whether he was the man or not—Glad win told me to go to the police-court, and give a description of the baggage.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman R 122). On 23rd Oct. I apprehended Hart, at Levy's direction, at his own house—I told him I took him on suspicion of stealing his master's luggage—he said nothing—I found the letter that has been produced in his knapsack, which was pointed out to me by the sergeant of the company—I took Hardy at 5, Grosvenor-mews, London, and told him I wanted him to come down to North Woolwich, to see if he could be identified as the person who removed the luggage on 8th October—he said, "Oh! I will go down with you"—while Hardy was among the crowd I said something to Gibbons; he pointed out Hardy—I asked Hart if Hardy was any relation of his, and he said he was his brother-in-law—I asked Hardy if Hart was any relation of his, and he said he was his brother-in-law.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you take Hardy? A. On 13th Nov. I had been to the same place where I took him, about three weeks before
—I did not search him, or the place—I asked him some questions, and he answered them—I searched the house afterwards, and found nothing connected with the robbery—( letter read—post mark 21st Sept. Thornton-hall, near Bedale, Yorkshire—"I shall be obliged if you will send all my things off as soon as possible, directed to me as above—I give you all my things, such as silver spoons, crockery, &c, which you packed up in the hamper —I shall only want all the boxes, tin boxes, gun-case, and carpet bag—you had better put my cloak in the sofa-box—I will give you my regimental frock coat, trowsers, and such things that I last wore, and left at the hotel—I will also give you the dog Skie, but the other one, Sailor, you must send with a ticket round his neck—the other dog you may sell or do what you like with—I also give you my tub and all the crockery in it, but I am afraid there is nothing worth having—send the baggage by train, to be left at Seeming station; write when you send them. I sincerely hope you will remember what I am about to say, which is, to be steady, and I have no doubt you will get on—you have my best wishes for your welfare and happiness in this world, and believe me yours truly,—M. S. Dodsworth. Directions to be put on luggage—'Captain Dodsworth, Thornton-hall, near Bedale, Yorkshire, to be left at Seeming station.'")
( Hardy received a good character.)
HART — GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Twelve Months.
HARDY — GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Eight Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18501125-161]
161. WILLIAM RHODES , stealing 30 sovereigns, 20 half-sovereigns, and 1 10 l. bank-note; the property of Robert Calton. ( See June Session.)
MR. COCKLE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT CATTON . I am a linen-draper's assistant—in April I lodged in Sutton-street, Soho. On 10th April I saw the prisoner about eleven o'clock in the morning, in the parlour of the Canterbury Arms, in Lambeth—he came in with Anderson—they both spoke to me—there was a game of skittles to be played between me and one of the name of Jones—the prisoner said he would take the chalk, and see that the game was played fair—I played one game for 3 l., and then Jones made me a bet of 50 l. that I could not knock the skittles down in nine throws—I said I would bet him 50 l.—I had not the 50 l. with me, and I went to 3, Sutton-street, Soho, where I lived, and asked George Williams, my landlord, to lend me 50 l.—I got thirty sovereigns, twenty half-sovereigns, and a 10 l.-bank-note—I returned to the Canterbury Arms with James Chick, who I had met in St. George's-road—he went with me to borrow the money, and returned with me to the Canterbury Arms—when I got back, it might be twelve or half-past twelve o'clock—I found the prisoner, Jones and Anderson, and one of the name of Strong in the parlour—they sent for some sausages, and two or three bottles of wine—we then went in the skittle-ground—I put down 40 l., and one of the party said, "You had better play for a bottle of wine before playing for the 50 l. "—I put down the 40 l. on the table—Chick was at the table—the prisoner was marking the chalks in the middle of the skittle-ground—the bottle of wine was brought in—
I played the game for the bottle of wine with Jones—the prisoner took the chalk—I lost, and then Jones made away, and Chick, Strong, and Anderson prevented me from going away—when I went out of the skittle-ground, I went to see if I could see anything of Jones—the prisoner remained in the skittle-ground with the other three—I returned to the skittle-ground afterwards, and they were all gone, and my money was gone—I had put down 40 l. in the commencement of the game, and Chick said, "I have got Jones's money, I might as well have yours"—I then put down the other 10 l.—after that, the bottle of wine was played for; I lost it, and Chick handed the money over to Jones as I suppose, and Jones got away—while this was going on, the prisoner was about half-way down the skittle-ground, and he was gone when I came back—I had not lost that money in the game that I played; I played for a bottle of wine—the bet was for 50 l.:I never played that game at all—I did not see the prisoner after that till now.
NOT GUILTY .

163. WILLIAM COMLEY , unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin: to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18501125-164]
164. GEORGE WILLIAMS was indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. CLERK and W. J. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ANN GIBBS . I am the daughter of Frederick Gibbs, a greengrocer at Brixton. On 15th Oct. the prisoner came for a penny red-herring, and gave me a bad half-crown—I took it up-stairs, and laid it on the table by the side of the till, and brought him down 2 s. 5 d. change—my father came in about an hour after, and I brought him down the half-crown.
Cross-examined by MR. M'MAHON. Q. What time was it? A. A quarter before eight o'clock in the morning; there was no person in the shop but the prisoner—he asked for nothing but the herring—he got it, and went away directly.
FREDERICK GIBBS . On the morning of 15th Oct., I came home from market about nine o'clock—my daughter gave me a half-crown—I put a mark on it, and gave it to Kelly.
JOHN KELLY ( police-sergeant, P 22). I produce the half-crown.
MARY ANN HILL . I am a widow, and live in Frederick-place, Brixton. On Tuesday, 22nd Oct., the prisoner followed me into my house, and asked for a pint of milk—it came to 2 d.—he gave me a half-crown—I turned it over, and said, "I think this is not a good one?"—he said, "Yes it is; you are a poor woman, and work hard for your living, and I do the same; I would not cheat you"—I gave him two shillings and a 4 d.-piece—I gave the half-crown to Freeman, the officer, and he bent it—I went after the prisoner, who was walking away from my house—I called to him, and he walked on the faster, and went up a turning on the opposite side—I held up my hand, and then Freeman got ahead of me, and stopped him.
DANIEL FREEMAN ( policeman, P 56). I live at Mrs. Hill's house. At a little before eight o'clock on the morning of 22nd Oct., I was coming
down-stairs—Mrs. Hill gave me a half-crown—I went out with her to the Brixton-road—I saw the prisoner about two hundred yards off; I followed and apprehended him—when Mrs. Hill called to him, he went on faster, and turned towards the Park-road—he made an attempt to get over the fence of a nursery-ground—he had a mug in his hand with milk in it—I told him he was charged with passing a bad half-crown—he said he did not know it was bad—he told Mrs. Hill he would pay her anything the liked for her milk—I found on him 1 l. 0 s. 10 d. in silver, 9 d. in copper, and a steel purse.
Cross-examined. Q. How far were you from him when he attempted to get over the fence? A. Not more than twenty yards; this is the half-crown, I have bent it.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These half-crowns are both bad.
Witnesses for the Defence.
PATRICK RYAN . I am a labourer, and live at Vauxhall. On 15th Oct. I was in the house that I lodged in last June—I had a sore leg, and was laid up ten or twelve days—the prisoner came to lodge there on 13th Oct., and went to work on the 14th for Mary Hayes—he was pulling down a copper and making it up—I saw him at work there on the 14th, on the 15th, and on the 16th—I breakfasted every day at eight o'clock— I breakfasted with him—he breakfasted with me on the 15th with the people of the house—I cannot tell how far that is from Brixton Church.
Cross-examined by MR. CLERK. Q. Did the prisoner sleep in the house where you did? A. Yes, and he had breakfast at eight o'clock in the morning—they went to work at six—Mrs. Hayes provided breakfast—the prisoner remained in that house from the 13th till the 17th, and since then I never saw him—on 15th Oct. he was at work in the kitchen—I was in the kitchen that morning between six and eight—I could not swear to the time, but I always used to come down to breakfast.
MR. M'MAHON. Q. How long before breakfast were you in the kitchen that morning? A. I cannot say, but I came down and saw him at work, and he remained at work till breakfast—the men breakfasted all together—he was at work that morning before breakfast—I never missed him during the time.
MARY HAYES . The prisoner came to lodge at my house on 13th Oct., at 8, Vauxhall-walk—that is a good way from Brixton Church—he worked on Monday and Tuesday, the 14th and 15th, and the 16th, and left me on the 17th—he was at work at half-past six o'clock on 15th Oct.—the men breakfasted with me—at eight o'clock I went out for a pound of suguar and some milk—I left him in my house when I went out at a quarter before eight, and found him there when I came back.
Cross-examined. Q. You are an Irishwoman? A. Yes; the prisoner is Irish too—eight men were lodging in my house, all Irishmen—they all breakfasted at home, some at half-past six, and some at eight—I went out at a quarter before eight—I have a clock; it goes right, I keep it regular for the men to go to work—I set it right myself—I was only gone five minutes to the bakehouse and the shop—the prisoner was in my house from the 14th till the 17th—I do not know where he went then.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-165]
165. PATRICK CONNELL and JOHN HARRINGTON were indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. CLERK and W. J PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JUDITH FEAR . I keep the Rising Sun beer-house. On 17th Oct. I saw the two prisoners in my house, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon—Harrington called for a pint of beer and a screw of tobacco—he gave me a bad shilling—I gave him 9 d. change—I put the shilling into the till—I had just taken all the silver out to pay my taxes, and had none there—a little after, Connell called for a pint of beer, and gave my young man, Gascoyne, a shilling—he immediately discovered it was bad, and he said to him, "You have given me a bad shilling"—Connell took it away from Gascoyne's hand—Gascoyne went to the till immediately to look what shilling I had taken, and he said, "You have taken a bad shilling"—I said, "Yes, I took it of Harrington"—the prisoners heard that—Gascoyne took it out and showed it to me, and then he showed it to Harrington—the officer was sent for, he gave him the shilling, and he took one from Connell—the prisoners were sitting together.
CHARLES GASCOYNE . I am potman to Mrs. Fear. I received a shilling from Connell for some beer—I told him it was bad—he said he took it from his master, and asked me to give it him back, which I did, and he put it into his pocket—I then asked my mistress if she had taken a bad shilling—I went to the till, took the shilling out, and said to Harrington, "You have given the lady a bad shilling"—he said he had not—I said he had, and he said, "I will give you good money for it"—he pulled out a sixpence, and then another sixpence—I gave the shilling I took out of the till, to the officer, and the officer was in the act of putting his hand into Connell's pocket, and Connell pulled out the shilling himself which he had tendered to me.
Connell. Q. Did anybody drink in company with us? A. Not that I saw—I did not see any woman there—I had never seen you with Harrington before, to my knowledge.
Harrington. Q. What time did I come into your house? A. A little before dusk—it was half an hour or three-quarters after my mistress had the shilling that I had the other.
MICHAEL TROY ( policeman, H 482). I was called and took the prisoners—I received one shilling from Gascoyne and another from Connell.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These shillings are both bad.
Connell's Defence. I was at work till half-past three o'clock, and I went for a pint of porter; if I had known the shilling was bad I had plenty of opportunity of making away with it.
Harrington's Defence. We left work, and there was singing in this house; we went and called for a pint of beer and paid for it.
CONNELL — GUILTY . Aged 30.
HARRINGTON — GUILTY . Aged 32.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-166]
166. MARY JOHNSON was indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. CLERK and W. J. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT EDWARD SWYER . I am a surgeon, and live in Kennington-lane. On 25th Oct. the prisoner came for a pennyworth of antibilious pills—she gave me a sixpence—I put it at the back of my till, because I
thought her appearance suspicious—I tried the sixpence, and saw it was bad, and asked her how she dared to bring bad money, knowing it was bad—she said she brought it from her master, Mr. Johnson—I asked where she lived—she said two turnings up; she did not know the name of the street, nor the number—she wanted my boy to go—I said the officer should go.
WILLIAM BUDD ( police-sergeant, L 49). I took the prisoner, and received this sixpence from Mr. Swyer—I asked the prisoner where she lived—she said two turnings from there—I knew there was but one turning, and I took her to the station—the searcher gave me this sixpence.
MARY SHERIDAN . As I was searching the prisoner at the Lambeth station I heard something fall from her dress—I saw a sixpence on the floor—I said, "You have dropped this sixpence from your hand"—she said, "I did not drop it from my hand; do not let them see it, I will be good to you when I go out; they do not know that I had it; they asked me whether I had any more, and it will be all the worse for me having that"—I gave the sixpence to the Inspector—he marked it in my presence, and gave it to the officer.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These are both counterfeit, and from the same mould.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-167]
167. ROBERT GISSING , unlawfully having counterfeit coin in his possession.
MESSRS. CLERK and W. J PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HANCKE . I live in Victoria-place, Clapham-road. On 15th Oct. I saw the prisoner and another lad at the bottom of the Lansdowne-road; they appeared in conversation—I saw the other boy in about five minutes come to the Landsdowne Arms for a quartern of gin—he tendered what appeared to be a shilling—the landlady refused it, and said it was bad—she took it in her hand and put it in the detector—the boy said he took it of a woman at the corner, with a black bonnet and a blue shawl—the potman and the boy went out to look for that woman, and I went with them—I saw the prisoner on the opposite side—I followed him till I lost sight of him at some new buildings—I turned up Larkhall-lane and over-took him—he came out from some building—I went and told him he must come with me to the Lansdowne Arms; he wanted to know what for—I told him I would tell him when I got him there—this was about twenty minutes before three o'clock—I do not know what became of the other boy—I believe the potman brought him back with the policeman.
Prisoner. Q. How long was I standing with the lad? A. You were standing talking with him on the right-hand side—the man who sweeps the crossing was there; I did not notice anybody else there—when you went alone you were trying to conceal yourself.
SAMUEL SIMPSON . On the afternoon of 15th Oct. I was in Sussex-place, Larkhall-lane, where there are some new buildings—I saw the prisoner there—he first tried to make a hole with his heel by the side of a well—after he had done that, I saw him throw something white into the well—I went to the well and got on a board—I saw the last witness catch the prisoner—I emptied the water out of the well and found seven bad shillings—they were not wrapped up, but were on the top of one another—I took them to the station—I afterwards found one shilling in the well—I marked that as I had done the others, and gave it to the officer
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear that I chucked them in? A. I can swear that you threw something white in, and went away—I did not put them down myself.
WILLIAM ANGOVE ( policeman, V 124). I went with the potman to the Lansdowne Arms, and took one lad; the landlord refused to prosecute him, and he was let go—I received this shilling from the potman.
ANN MOUSLEY . The shilling that was given to this officer, was the one that I received from the boy.
JOHN MARSHALL ( police-sergeant, V 12). I produce seven bad shillings, which I received from Simpson.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . This shilling that was uttered, and the seven found in the well, are all bad, and from the same mould.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18501125-168]
168. THOMAS RODLEY , stealing 1 skittle-ball, value 5 s., the goods of James Loughton; having been before convicted.
MR. MAGUIRE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES LOUGHTON . I am landlord of the Rose, in Tower-street, Waterloo-road, Lambeth. On 7th Nov. I met the prisoner going out at my back-door as I was coming in—I said there was no one there—he said he wanted to go backwards, and I suffered him to go—I had observed that my skittle-ball was safe when I came out of the yard—I had laid it down myself, and about eleven that night I missed it—I went after the prisoner and found him next door to a coffee-shop in the Waterloo-road—he said he knew nothing about it—I gave him in charge, and from some information I went the next day, between one and two, to Mr. Butcher's, at the Great Britain—I went into his skittle-ground and saw two skittle-balls—one of them I was confident was mine—this is it( produced)—here is a mark on one side and one on the other—I told Mr. Butcher it was mine, and he told me where he bought it—I went to Mr. Lockett's, at the Crown and Anchor, and asked him how he became possessed of it, and he told me.
Prisoner. Q. When I asked to go backwards did I not go? A. I do not know—I did not see you come out of the house—I was away on my business—I am sure no one went into my yard besides you—there are plenty of ways to go into my yard.
CHARLES LOCKETT . I was waiter at the Crown and Anchor in the Waterloo-road. On 7th Nov. I saw the prisoner there—I said I had been trying to sell a skittle-ball to Mr. Butcher, but it was not large enough—the prisoner said he had a ball about the size that Mr. Butcher wanted—he went and fetched it—I believe this is it—I asked him what he wanted for it—he said two shillings, and all I could get over that I might keep myself—I took it to Mr. Butcher's—he gave me 2 s. 3 d. and a pint of ale for it—I gave the prisoner 2 s. and kept the 3 d.
JONATHAN LARTER ( policeman, L 106). I went to a coffee-shop on 8th Nov. and took the prisoner—he said he knew nothing about the ball.
Prisoner. I was taken once, and had three months, since then I have got my living honestly, as the officer knows. Witness. I have seen him in the neighbourhood of the New-cut—I do not know how he has got his living.
JESSE RUTLAND ( police-sergeant, L 28). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read- Thomas Owen, Convicted July, 1850; Confined three months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
